- **Symptom:** `[RED] BindVertexAttribState n=17`, tens of thousands of times, and a scene that draws wrong or not at all: the bind was refused, so the draws kept the previous layout.
- **Cause:** NVN has 16 vertex attributes and NVN2 32, and every table in the host held 16.
- **Fix:** the attribute tables hold 32 (`p_bind_vattr`, the replay's state), and so does the answer to the device query. The pipeline key keeps the first 16 where they were and adds `attr_hi` at its end, so a prewarm row written before reads as 0 (`pso_skey_parse`); `dmsh_vin.py` leaves out such shaders (its shadows take Metal attributes 16–30), and they take the ordinary path; `dmsh_pull.py` makes their pulling libraries, since its layout block holds all 32 attributes. Vertex bindings stay 16.
- **Found on:** [Yoshi and the Mysterious Book](../../dm/games/0400245021D2C000/README.md): Unreal Engine 5.4's vertex factories use attribute 16 on stream 6.

### Vertex formats Metal lacks

- **Symptom:** `vertex attribute format … not mapped` (printed once).
- **Cause / Fix:** Mario uses `RGB10A2SN` (0x69) → `MTLVertexFormatInt1010102Normalized`. Unmapped formats fall back to a 4-component read and print the line once. The integer-read-as-float formats (`*_UI2F` / `*_I2F`, 0x51–0x68) have no Metal vertex format of their own: 8- and 16-bit ones are read by [Metal's own fetch](#integer-to-float-vertex-formats-converted-on-the-cpu), 32-bit ones and the routes without it are converted on the CPU at replay like the other conversions (`vconv_i2f`).
- **Found on:** [Super Mario Bros. Wonder](../../dm/games/010015100B514000/README.md) (`RGB10A2SN`); [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md) has an `RG8_UI2F` attribute.

### Integer-to-float vertex formats converted on the CPU

- **Symptom:** the `vertex attribute format … (integers read as floats …): converted on the CPU at replay` line, and the `vertex conversion:` line counts vertices a submission for streams of 8- or 16-bit `*_UI2F` / `*_I2F` attributes (0x51–0x68 but the 32-bit 0x55, 0x56, 0x5B, 0x5C, 0x61, 0x62, 0x67, 0x68); the picture is right, the submit thread pays for it.
- **Cause:** `vconv_wanted` sent every integer-read-as-float attribute to the CPU conversion (into the frame arena every submission, or a [kept conversion](#kept-vertex-conversions)), and `vconv_fill` decoded those formats element by element through `vconv_elem`. Metal's fetch already does that conversion: an 8- or 16-bit integer vertex format (`UChar2`, `Short4` …) read into a `float` input gives the value as a float (200 → 200.0), as Maxwell's fetch does for these formats; only 32-bit ones are refused into a float input.
- **Fix:** in `nvn_mtl.m`, for every NVN game. `vin_on` gives a pipeline its routes (`VIN_I2F`, `VIN_SHADOW`), asked by `pso_desc` and `vconv_bind` alike so the pipeline's buffer slots and the bound conversions agree: an 8- or 16-bit integer-read-as-float attribute feeds a float input, or the float declaration of an integer input's [shadow inputs](#shadow-vertex-inputs), directly with the integer format of its layout (`vfmt_for_ct` keeps it). 32-bit ones, an integer input without a usable float shadow, transform feedback / geometry / discard captures (and the geometry hazard check, which asks with no routes) and `DM_NVN_VCONV_CPU=1` keep the CPU conversion, whose loop now decodes the format once per call. Ordinary integer formats into a float input keep their register bits through the CPU, as before. No shader chain change. `pso_offline.py` (`i2f_native`, `vin_ct`, `vconv_wanted`) makes the bundled layout bases with the same descriptors, so an archive made before this change misses these layouts and the device builds them. `DM_NVN_I2F_NATIVE=0` converts them all on the CPU again (kept in player builds, for the phone A/B; that side builds such layouts on the device, not from the bundle). Adapted from ComicBit/nxnative `39cc2e79c` (MIT), which upstream NXNative has not taken.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests check_t_i2f_fetch`: all 24 formats into float, uint and int inputs, with and without a float shadow and as captures, through the production `pso_desc`; through the native route the words the vertex shader receives equal the CPU conversion's bit for bit for every 8-bit and every 16-bit value in each component, with Metal's API validation; `vconv_fill` equals `vconv_elem` for every format (unaligned source, odd stride, stride 0); then again with `DM_NVN_I2F_NATIVE=0` and `DM_NVN_VCONV_CPU=1`. `test_pso_offline.py` and `t_pso_bundle` check the bundled bases: an 8-bit one through native fetch, a 32-bit one through the conversion slot, a uint input through its float shadow and one without a shadow library.
- **Found on:** ComicBit's fork, on [The Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom](../../dm/games/0100F2C0115B6000/README.md#found-on-this-game) (theirs: format 0x64, `RGBA8_I2F`, at attribute 1 of four vertex shaders on Great Sky Island; Mac only). NX 18 has not measured it on a game or a device; [Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md)'s `RG8_UI2F` attribute takes the same route.

### Long vertex ranges in the CPU conversion

- **Symptom:** `[RED] vertex attribute conversion: a range of 915138 vertices exceeds the per-frame staging (format 0x14)` and geometry stretched into streaks from the picture's centre (a stadium's stands and roof), while the pitch, the HUD and the characters draw right.
- **Cause:** the CPU conversion (`vconv_bind`) refused a range above 4 MiB of staging, 262,144 vertices of four 32-bit values. The attribute was then left unconverted: Maxwell's fetch gives the shader the integers as float32 bits, and the bytes were read as they were. 8- and 16-bit integer formats feeding float inputs always take this path (red line 99; [shadow vertex inputs](#shadow-vertex-inputs) cover integer inputs only).
- **Fix:** the conversion takes up to 64 MiB, 4,194,304 vertices (`arena_alloc` makes an arena of its own above 4 MiB, which `ARENA_IDLE` releases), and a range that long is normally converted once and kept ([Kept vertex conversions](#kept-vertex-conversions)).
- **Found on:** [EA SPORTS FC 27](../../dm/games/0100C49025D3E000/README.md), by the author of PR #1726: the Estadio da Luz at the 1.8d.6523 update, a stream of 915,138 `UI16 x2` vertices. Not run here (no files of that update). Other grounds, or any mesh with a stream longer than 262,144 vertices, may be affected.

### Kept vertex conversions

- **Symptom:** the `vertex conversion:` line reports millions of vertices per submission and the submitting thread's translation takes most of a frame: 7.65 million vertices, 122 MB written every frame, 26.8 ms on an M4 (about 37 fps) in the stadium above, as PR #1727 measured.
- **Cause:** the CPU conversion was made again in every submission, whether or not the source had changed, and a stadium's meshes do not change.
- **Fix:** a conversion is kept across submissions while its source is unchanged (`vk_get` in `nvn_mtl.m`). A key (range, format, layout) is noted at its first submission with 64 samples of its source, and converted into a buffer of its own at the next one that finds the samples unchanged (a stream the game rewrites is then never kept; a change the samples miss is found by the checks below). The pages wholly inside the source are write-protected (`nvn_dirty_protect`, as a kept index range); the bytes before and after them, in pages shared with other data, are compared with a copy, so a neighbour written every frame neither drops the conversion nor takes a fault for it. A GPU write queued on the source (copy, update, fill, transform feedback, a writable storage binding: `vk_gpu_write`) drops it, and none may be queued or in flight when it is made. A changed source releases its buffer once the submissions that read it complete, and is tried again after 32 submissions, twice as many after each further change, so a stream rewritten every frame costs a write-protection that often.
- **Note:** not kept: ranges below 4,096 vertices, sources outside tracked pools, anything while the game has sparse mappings (a write through an alias takes no fault), and conversions past 256 MB in all, on iOS near the memory limit too: a conversion refused is made again in every submission that draws it, which holds more memory than keeping it. A key not drawn for 600 submissions is forgotten, and going to the background drops every kept conversion ([Kept vertex conversions held while the app is in the background](memory.md#kept-vertex-conversions-held-while-the-app-is-in-the-background)). `DM_NVN_VCONV_KEEP=0` turns it off (`=n` sets the smallest range kept). The `vertex conversion:` line gives the vertices bound from kept conversions, the conversions held and their size, and how many were made and dropped, by cause. `t_vconv_keep` checks the content against `vconv_elem`, each way a conversion is dropped or left, and that a low headroom does not refuse one.
- **Note:** PR #1727 proposed converting long ranges on every core instead (26.8 → 10.6–12.7 ms on that M4). That still writes the 122 MB every frame and takes every core beside the game's threads; it was not taken. A fetch on the GPU (a shadow input for float inputs) would also serve streams that do change.
- **Found on:** [EA SPORTS FC 27](../../dm/games/0100C49025D3E000/README.md) (the measurement above is the PR author's; the stadium was not run here with this change, so its effect there is open). Run here on [Fire Emblem: Three Houses](../../dm/games/010055D009F78000/README.md) with `DM_NVN_VCONV_CPU=1`: on the note's new-game route into the prologue battle (M4 Pro), the battle's bound ranges came to about 2.0 million vertices a submission, of which 1.8 million were bound from 144 kept conversions (18 MB) and 0.2 million converted; frame time p50 16.68 ms with and without, p99 17.2–17.6 against 20.4 ms, the same picture at 450 s. That run was made before the samples were added: in the cutscenes it made and dropped about 1.2 conversions a frame, nearly all of streams the game rewrites, which the samples are there to leave alone. The form with the samples passes `t_vconv_keep` and was not run in a game.

### Null vertex buffers bound past stream 15

- **Symptom:** `[RED] BindVertexBuffers first=16 n=1` … `first=31`, thousands of times a run and no visible fault.
- **Cause:** [Mario Tennis Fever](../../dm/games/0400D4201C6EA000/README.md) clears 32 streams before each model, while NVN has 16.
- **Fix:** a null range past stream 15 is dropped silently, as nothing can read it (`hle_nvn.c`); a real one still prints a red line, limited to 8.
- **Found on:** [Mario Tennis Fever](../../dm/games/0400D4201C6EA000/README.md).

### Vertex streams 14 and 15 at Metal slots 14 and 15

- **Symptom:** the assertion `buffer index (31) must be < 31`.
- **Cause:** the host bound NVN stream *n* to Metal vertex buffer 16 + *n*, but Metal has slots 0–30 only (30 is the host's constant fallback), so stream 15 went to 31.
- **Fix:** translated shaders use only `buffer(0)`, so streams 14 and 15 now go to slots 14 and 15 (`VB_SLOT`).
- **Found on:** [13 Sentinels: Aegis Rim](../../dm/games/01003FC01670C000/README.md) (a minute and a half into the prologue).

### Vertex streams at addresses that are not 4-byte aligned

- **Symptom:** Scaleform fills come out as solid white or light grey sheets over the whole screen, hard-edged triangles and bands, and the menu text is missing (a language list or a title screen that is all white behind its button prompt).
- **Cause:** Metal reads a vertex buffer only from a 4-byte-aligned address: bound at an address 2 mod 4, every vertex was read 2 bytes early. NVN takes any address, and Scaleform's vertex streams (16-byte vertices: two RGBA8 attributes and an RG32F position) sit at addresses such as `…6d6a`. The positions became garbage spanning the screen, and the shapes, white with alpha 0.2–0.7 by their colour transforms, overlapped many times.
- **Fix:** `bind_vbufs` copies a stream whose address is not 4-byte aligned into the submission's arena (`vbuf_unaligned`; one copy per address and size until guest writes or pool mappings change, up to 64 MB) and binds the copy. CPU copies require a host-readable, non-sparse range without pending GPU writers in this or an earlier submission; otherwise `vbuf_prepare` encodes an ordered GPU copy before the render encoder, including feedback and geometry captures. Submissions and pool mappings invalidate copies; buffer and shader writes invalidate the copies they can overlap, so unrelated uniform updates keep their vertex copies. The log's `vertex bindings` line counts the copies; `t_vbuf` checks byte-aligned readbacks, reuse, queued writes, writes between draws and pool replacement. The index buffers at addresses 2 mod 4 (16-bit indices) needed nothing.
- **Found on:** Batman: Arkham Knight (`0100ACD0163D0000`; its profile and note are in a PR of their own) (Scaleform GFx; 2–13 copies of 3–18 MB a frame in the menus and at the title, which the draws' vertex ranges could narrow). With the copy the language menu and the title screen draw right.

### Vertex fetches past a stream's bound size

- **Symptom:** hard-edged translucent slabs and triangles over the scene that move with the camera, where a full-screen pass blends an effect in (Super Mario Odyssey: white wedges through the Cascade Kingdom's waterfall mist, one edge through the centre of the screen).
- **Cause:** NVN binds each vertex stream with a size (`nvnCommandBufferBindVertexBuffers`), and Maxwell's fetch reads zeros past it: a draw may ask for more vertices than the stream holds, and those vertices collapse into degenerate triangles. Metal's fetch has no such limit and read on into whatever followed the stream in the pool. agl's half-resolution composite draws `DrawArrays(TRIANGLES, 0, 9)` from a 36-byte stream holding one full-screen triangle; vertices 6–8 read (0, 0), (0, 1), (0.98, 0.20) from the pool's next bytes, a second triangle that blended the mist in twice. A numpy model of the composite from the frame's inputs matched the GPU's output everywhere but there, where the output matched the composite applied twice (1 % against 39 % error).
- **Fix:** before the draw's render encoder, `vbuf_prepare` takes the inputs the vertex shader reads and the draw's last element per stream (`vstream_last`: the last vertex of a direct draw, the last instance of a per-instance stream, element 0 of a stride-0 stream), and where that reaches past the bound size (`vbuf_need`, element sizes from `vattr_bytes`), binds the stream as a copy of its bound bytes followed by zeros up to the last byte read. The copy is `vbuf_unaligned`'s (per submission, CPU or ordered GPU copy); the vertex table, the pulling pipelines' layout and transform feedback take it, and the CPU-converted inputs (`vconv_bind`) convert zero bytes past the bound range. `t_vbuf` reads the zeros back on the GPU; `t_pull` checks `vattr_bytes` against Metal's element sizes.
- **Open:** per-vertex streams of indexed draws, indirect draws and the batched geometry captures are not bounded (their last element is not known on the CPU without scanning the indices or reading the arguments).
- **Absent streams (resolved, found by clang-tidy):** CPU conversion already binds zero for a stream outside the sixteen stream slots, but its range query indexed `S.stream` first. `vstream_last` now returns unknown before accessing an absent slot; `t_vbuf` checks both boundary indices. No game failure was observed.
- **Found on:** [Super Mario Odyssey](../../dm/games/0100000000010000/README.md) (GitHub issue #558): the composite of agl's half-resolution translucent pass (`alProgramShader`, vertex `40cc99352976d0ff`, pixel `934386ce027e72be`), every frame the half-resolution effects draw.

### A vertex stream in no memory pool

- **Symptom:** `[RED] vertex stream N's buffer 0x… is in no memory pool; draw skipped` (`; its attributes read zeros` for a pulling pipeline, `; a geometry capture reads the buffer bound before` for one). Before the line existed: a draw whose vertices came from an earlier draw's buffer, with nothing in the log.
- **Cause:** the host failed to place a non-zero stream address in any memory pool it tracks (a pool it missed, or an address the game computed wrongly; on the Switch the GPU would fault). `bind_vbufs` skipped such a stream, so its Metal slot kept the buffer an earlier draw had bound there. A stream with address 0 is different: the vertex descriptor points its attributes at the zero buffer (`ZERO_ATTR`).
- **Fix:** `bind_vbufs` reports the first stream the vertex shader reads (`vs_streams`, from its reflection) that is in no pool, after binding the others. The draw is skipped with the red line, as for an index buffer in no pool, and so are a transform feedback capture and the vertex dump; a pulling pipeline's layout gives the stream's attributes zeros. A geometry capture shares its encoder with the job's later stages, which read what it writes, so it runs with the red line and the old binding. A stale address on a stream no input reads changes nothing. Not a fix for the address itself: the red line is there to find which pool the host missed.
- **Found on:** no game has shown the line. The S2Arm project reported the stale binding on its copy of this layer. `t_vbuf` checks that a read stream in no pool is reported and the others still bound, and that an unread one is not.

### A vertex stream bound past its memory pool

- **Symptom:** draws missing although their vertices are in a pool: South Park: The Fractured But Whole's character preview, its loading indicator, and a title that flickers between black and drawn frames. The log may show one `[RED] vertex stream N's buffer 0x… is in no memory pool; draw skipped`, then later draws skipped the same way silently (a pass dump shows `Z … draw skipped: stream N range …+… has no mapped vertex binding`).
- **Cause:** Snowdrop binds a stream from an offset inside a buffer with the buffer's whole size. The declared range runs past the end of its pool while every vertex a draw fetches is in it, and the lookup asked for the whole declared range, failed, and the draw was skipped ([A vertex stream in no memory pool](#a-vertex-stream-in-no-memory-pool)).
- **Fix:** `nvn_mtl.m`, ported from NXNative c96c45e9b and 79555b676 (MIT). When the whole-range lookup of an aligned stream the shader reads fails, `bind_vbufs` and the pulling layout (`pull_layout_fill`) try the prefix the draw reads (`vbuf_draw_bytes`): the last element from the draw (`vstream_last`), or for a direct indexed draw from its indices and base vertex (`vbuf_index_last`), which must be in a pool, readable, with no pending copy, update or fill, no GPU write over them and no sparse mapping, and no negative index. Every reflected attribute on the stream must have a known width. Such a binding holds for that draw only (not cached), bounds the draw's format conversion (`vconv_bind`), and the whole-range cache now keys on the size too. Draws with unknown bounds, indirect ones and fetches past the mapped bytes still fail. Differences from NXNative: the index readability check is `mem_readable` and its GPU-write check `gw_live` (NX 18 has no `nvn_host_readable` or `hw_sub_hits`); the lookups (`bind_vbufs`) take the draw at every caller, the transform feedback capture and the vertex dump included.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_vbuf`: a short binding is cached; widened past the pool, an indirect draw is rejected, a direct draw binds the prefix (conversion limited to it, pulling too) and reads its vertex on the GPU; a longer draw is rejected and the prefix is not reused; a direct indexed draw binds the prefix its largest index plus base vertex needs, and is rejected when that reaches past the pool, with a negative index, or with a pending copy over its indices. `t_pull` and `t_i2f_fetch` still pass.
- **Found on:** South Park: The Fractured But Whole on NXNative (`01008F2005154000`, no profile in NX 18; ported, not run here).

### Programs bound per stage

- **Symptom:** scenes and characters black, only foliage, light shafts and the HUD drawn; pass dumps full of `Z … draw skipped: no program bound`.
- **Cause:** NVN's `BindProgram(program, stageBits)` binds per stage, and a null program unbinds just those stages. Metroid Dread's depth prepass calls `BindProgram(program, vertex | fragment)`, then `BindProgram(null, fragment)`, and draws depth with the vertex shader alone. The host replaced its single bound program on every bind, so a null bind meant "no program bound", and all of the prepass's ~140 draws per frame were dropped. The depth buffer stayed empty, and the G-buffer passes, which test depth `EQUAL` without writing, passed no pixel.
- **Fix:** the replay tracks the program per stage (`bind_stages` in `nvn_mtl.m`). When the stages come from different programs, or the fragment stage is unbound, it builds a composite program, which gives a vertex-only pipeline for the prepass. Games that always bind every stage from one program (Mario, Engage) are unchanged.
- **Found on:** [Metroid Dread](../../dm/games/010093801237C000/README.md): the combinations it uses are (program, 0x3), (null, 0x2), (null, 0x7). [13 Sentinels: Aegis Rim](../../dm/games/01003FC01670C000/README.md) builds one program per GLSLC output and pairs them only through these composites ([Single-stage programs paired with a cover](shaders.md#single-stage-programs-paired-with-a-cover)).
- **Note:** pass dumps print a `Z` line wherever a draw is dropped (no program, no pipeline, empty scissor, both faces culled, no render pass). That is how this one was found: 144 `Z … draw skipped: no program bound` lines in a frame whose G-buffer pass had draws but stayed black.

### Single-stage programs bound with every graphics bit

- **Symptom:** a black screen from boot while the game keeps running (its frame loop issues draws every frame); `[RED] program has no vertex shader (or it is not mapped)` once; GPU time 0.0 ms/s.
- **Cause:** the game makes one program per shader (`ProgramSetShaders` with one stage) and binds each with all graphics bits: `BindProgram(vertex program, 0x1f)`, then `BindProgram(pixel program, 0x1f)`, then the draw. `bind_stages` gave every stage in the bits to the bound program, so the pixel program's bind also replaced the vertex slot. Every draw then had a program without a vertex shader, and `pso_get_` dropped it. On the hardware, binding a non-null pixel-only (or geometry/tessellation-only) graphics program with `0x1f` leaves the vertex stage intact, whereas fragment, geometry, tessellation and mesh stages without a shader in the bound program are still unbound ([Xenoblade Chronicles 2](../../dm/games/0100E95004038000/README.md) binds vertex-only programs with `0x1f` after VS+FS; [Mario Kart World](../../dm/games/0400C3F00006E000/README.md) and [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) bind NVN2 mesh and depth-only task/mesh programs with `0xff` / `0xc3`).
- **Fix:** `bind_stages` (`nvn_mtl.m`) clears the vertex bit only for a non-null graphics program whose known stages (`prog_stage_mask`, read from `NvProg.stages` or `g_shmap` without calling `shader_get`) include another graphics stage and neither a vertex nor an NVN2 mesh shader, and have no unresolved stage. Fragment, geometry, tessellation, NVN2 mesh, compute-only, partially unmapped and `NULL` binds still update or unbind every bit they name. Ported from NXNative e59c910ad (MIT).
- **Found on:** upstream, Little Busters! Converted Edition (PR #3055): 51 single-stage programs, every bind `0x1f`.
- **Check:** `t_bind_stages` (CPU only, ASan/UBSan) covers the single-stage pair, the vertex-only, geometry, mesh, domain-only, compute-only and `NULL` binds, and the shader-map-only resolution.

### Conditional rendering survives queue continuations

- **Symptom:** a draw disabled by `SetRenderEnable(false)` executes after an event or fence, or a conditional clear erases a target whose preceding timestamp report should make it skip.
- **Cause:** physical submission initialization reset the global render-enable state and discarded known pending counter values. A logical NVN queue can continue across several Metal submissions, including [event waits](nvn-objects.md#gpu-event-waits-order-work-across-queues); those boundaries do not reset its render state. Timestamp reports are written to guest memory at GPU completion, so reading their old CPU bytes before that completion also gives the wrong predicate.
- **Fix:** render-enable state belongs to the logical queue. Known timestamp counter values survive physical prefixes until their completion writes land. Completion expires that knowledge before an event release can wake a CPU writer; a later CPU rewrite therefore remains visible. Later inline updates, fills and copies replace overlapping counter bytes and carry the latest writer’s completion fence. Copy sources with unfinished or unknown GPU writes remain unknown. This preserves the existing conservative treatment of unavailable occlusion-query results.
- **Check:** `t_render_enable_queue` uses the public NVN API and pixel readback to cover event, signal, sync and fence boundaries, independent queues, pending reports behind a GPU gate, and a later CPU counter rewrite. Seven checks failed before the fix; all 38 pass in both encoding modes with Metal validation. `t_render_counter_overlap` adds checks for explicit updates, fills, copies, partial writes and a second fence; five predicates failed before the metadata fix and all pass in both encoding modes. Both tests turn the profile's `render_cond` on themselves, whatever profile `GAME` names: built for Super Mario Bros. Wonder (the profile CI names), which has it off, the conditions were ignored and they failed every run. This does not resolve the older CPU-post report emulation: a later GPU read can still observe the report’s previous bytes, and a late CPU report write can overwrite a later GPU write. The fixture prints those bytes as OPEN lines and checks the predicates (37 checks): it had checked them, and failed on the race in 3 of 60 runs.
- **Open:** reports written on the GPU in command order (a counter heap, a resolve and a kernel at the end of each submission, keeping each game's time values) were built and measured: correct bytes in every case, but about 0.5 to 0.7 ms more GPU a frame on Ocarina of Time (about 14 submissions a frame) and no game symptom traced to the order, so not landed. The patch is kept outside the repository; each report's own GPU time instead of the segment times broke Ocarina's dynamic resolution ([GPU timestamps per submission segment](frame-rate.md#gpu-timestamps-per-submission-segment)). The game note records visual validation.
- **Found on:** the queue-ordering investigation for [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#open), M4 Pro.

### Composite program taken again after a replay state restore

- **Symptom:** `[RED] failed to create render pipeline (vertex 790573a52b9c5053_b fragment f39fb42850f50ecd_b): Fragment input(s) user(locn0),user(locn1) … not written by vertex shader`, once on entering the first investigation; `texture type mismatch: shader declares a cube, texture is 2D` (a pixel shader run with another pair's bindings).
- **Cause:** a draw ran with the vertex shader of another Shader object (`Legacy Shaders/VertexLit`'s shadow caster with the pixel shader of `PSYNC/BG_AlphaTest`, whose own pass pairs it with `00b462fe0dec6e06`). Unity binds the vertex and fragment programs apart, so every draw's pair is a composite of `bind_stages`, one of a ring of 64. The precompile prediction (`pso_predict_rest`, at a submission's first pipeline miss) saves the replay state, replays the rest of the submission's binds and restores it; in a 280-draw submission it made more than 64 composites, reused the slot the saved `S.prog` named, and the draws after it up to the next bind ran with that slot's new pair. The fusion probe and redraw and the deferred-draw replay restore `S` from copies the same way.
- **Fix:** each restore takes the composite again from the per-stage bindings it restored (`prog_resolve`, logged as `combined program taken again after a state restore`); a draw already under way keeps its `NvProg`, whose release is deferred. Every game.
- **Found on:** [AI: THE SOMNIUM FILES](../../dm/games/010089B00D09C000/README.md) (Unity 2017.4).
- **Check:** two runs each from one save with one key script, prewarm off: the old host logged the `texture type mismatch` line both times, the fixed host no red line, with the slot taken again twice.

### Compute programs bound apart from graphics programs

- **Symptom:** `no program bound before the draw`, `program has no vertex shader` (a draw after a dispatch is dropped); `the dispatched program has no compute shader` (a dispatch after a graphics bind).
- **Cause:** `BindProgram(program, stageBits)` with the compute bit replaced the one bound program, so a draw after a dispatch found the compute program (or none), and a dispatch after a graphics bind found no compute shader.
- **Fix:** the replay keeps the compute program apart (`S.cprog` in `nvn_mtl.m`, and `g_pp_cprog` in the pass analysis).
- **Found on:** [Hyrule Warriors: Age of Calamity](../../dm/games/01002B00111A2000/README.md): the game interleaves dispatches and draws; its intro movie's NV12 → RGB draw was dropped and the movie played black under its "+ Skip" prompt.

### Instanced array draws

- Instanced array draws (`DrawArraysInstanced`) carry instance count and base instance in the `OP_DRAW` record.
- **Found on:** [Super Mario Bros. Wonder](../../dm/games/010015100B514000/README.md).

### 8-bit index buffers

- **Cause:** Metal has only 16/32-bit indices.
- **Fix:** 8-bit indices are widened to 16-bit at replay into the frame arena.
- **Found on:** [Super Mario Bros. Wonder](../../dm/games/010015100B514000/README.md): its full-screen passes and its final copy use them.

### 8-bit indices widened on the GPU after a write in the same submission

- **Symptom:** a red line: the 8-bit index buffer had GPU-side writes in the submission (indices read stale).
- **Cause:** Metal has no 8-bit index type, so replay widens 8-bit indices to 16-bit on the CPU. When a copy, update or fill encoded earlier in the submission covers them (`pend_hits`), the CPU reads the bytes from before that write.
- **Fix:** those draws are widened on the GPU (`wide_run`): a compute pass reads the indices from a 16-byte-aligned address, with the byte shift as a constant, and writes 16-bit ones into the frame arena. As in `ixc_run`, the first such draw widens every such draw after it up to the next buffer write outside a render pass, so only a draw met inside an open render pass with no widening yet would split it. Draws whose indices no write covers keep the CPU widening. Log line `8-bit index GPU expansion`.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom](../../dm/games/0100F2C0115B6000/README.md): about one draw per widening, up to a dozen widenings a frame on the title and load screens, no pass split.

### Triangle fans and indexed quads

- **Cause:** Metal has neither triangle fans nor quads.
- **Fix:** non-indexed fans draw through a shared `(0, i, i+1)` index buffer with `first` as the base vertex; indexed fans and quads are expanded on the CPU into a 32-bit triangle list in the frame arena at replay (`nvn_mtl.m`, as for 8-bit indices).
- **Found on:** [13 Sentinels: Aegis Rim](../../dm/games/01003FC01670C000/README.md).

### Polygons drawn as triangle fans

- **Symptom:** `[RED] primitive type 9 not implemented (vertex … hull - domain - fragment …; draw skipped)`: whole draws missing (not tessellation: the hull and domain columns are `-`).
- **Cause:** 9 is `NVN_DRAW_PRIMITIVE_POLYGON`. Metal has no polygon primitive, and the replay's primitive switch had no case for it, so every such draw was skipped.
- **Fix:** the recorder turns it into a triangle fan (`prim_in` in `hle_nvn.c`, for `rec_draw`, `rec_draw_idx` and `DrawTransformFeedback`): a convex polygon v0..vn-1 is the fan (v0, vi, vi+1), the same coverage, as GL defines it. From there the fan paths of [Triangle fans and indexed quads](#triangle-fans-and-indexed-quads) take it (shared index buffer, CPU expansion, `ixc_run` for indirect ones, primitive restart).
- **Found on:** the S2Arm project reports 16 such draws in a Mac run of 0100F43008C44000 (ported from S2Arm dd8dbbaa); no game on the Mac's drives was run with it here.
- **Check:** `t_nvn` (`polygon draws recorded as fans`, compact and canonical records, every draw call; quads keep theirs). The fan drawing itself: `t_primitive_restart`.

### Quad strips drawn as triangle strips

- **Symptom:** `[RED] primitive type 8 not implemented (vertex … hull - domain - fragment …; draw skipped)`: whole draws missing.
- **Cause:** 8 is `NVN_DRAW_PRIMITIVE_QUAD_STRIP`. Metal has no quad strip, and the replay's primitive switch had no case for it.
- **Fix:** `prim_in` in `hle_nvn.c` records it as a triangle strip (5): GL's quad strip draws quads v0 v1 v3 v2, v2 v3 v5 v4 …, the same coverage as those vertices drawn as a triangle strip. Indexed, non-indexed and indirect draws all take the existing strip path. A direct draw's odd last vertex, which GL ignores, is dropped from the count (it would add a triangle to the strip); an indirect one's count is read by the GPU and keeps it (not seen).
- **Found on:** ported from S2Arm f899177a (Apache-2.0), which reports such draws in two games' diagnostics; no game here was run with it.
- **Check:** `t_nvn` (`quad strips recorded as triangle strips`, compact and canonical records, arrays, elements and indirect elements).

### Lines wider than a pixel

- **Symptom:** lines a game draws with `SetLineWidth` above 1 are hairlines (Mario Golf: Super Rush's aiming and putting grids, width 4 and 5, upstream). The host logged `SetLineWidth 4.00: Metal has only 1-pixel lines`.
- **Cause:** Metal draws lines 1 pixel wide and has no line width; the host dropped the width.
- **Fix:** `SetLineWidth` is recorded (`OP_LINE_WIDTH`, capture layout `op 59 p_line_width 4`, appended so older captures stay readable) and kept in the replay's state. A draw of lines, a line loop or a line strip whose width rounds to 2 or more is drawn as a quad per segment (`nvn_wline.inc`), as OpenGL draws a non-antialiased wide line and as Maxwell does: a parallelogram w pixels across the segment's minor axis in window space, its ends on the major axis, so segments meet without gaps or overlap.
  - It is the game's draw itself, in its own render pass, with the vertex shader's wide-line entry (`dmsh_pull.py`, `<entry>_wl`, in the pulling library) and 6 vertices a segment. Each vertex finds its segment's two endpoints from its vertex ID and a line block: their places by the primitive, their vertex IDs from the first vertex, or from the index buffer, read on the GPU, plus the base vertex.
  - It runs the shader's body for both endpoints, keeps its own endpoint's outputs and moves its position by half the width, scaled by its w. The segment is clipped first, to w > 0 and to the near plane when depth clipping is on, its outputs interpolated as the clipper would.
  - With multisampling on, a segment is the rectangle OpenGL draws then: w pixels across the segment, as long as it.
  - The fragment shader, blending, depth and stencil are the draw's own; culling is off and the polygon offset is the one enabled for lines. A slice-merged pass ends before a wide line (its pipeline is not amplified); pass-as-compute and face-split passes take triangles only, so a line never reaches them.
  - Indirect arguments are read on the CPU after a wait for the GPU work that may write them, as the geometry path reads its own. A restart index drops the segments it touches; an indexed line loop with restart on stays 1 pixel wide.
  - Stays 1 pixel wide, with `[RED] wide line … drawn 1 pixel wide: <reason>`: a vertex shader without the entry (no vertex inputs, or a parameter or output the entry cannot pass on), unreadable indirect arguments. Programs with a geometry or tessellation stage, transform feedback or rasterizer discard keep their own paths, 1 pixel wide. The width is capped at 64. `DM_NVN_LINE_WIDTH=0` draws every line 1 pixel wide.
  - **Cost:** only line draws wider than a pixel take this path. The body runs twice a corner, 12 times a segment, against twice for a 1-pixel line, and each such draw's pipeline is built on the submit thread at first use (no archive entry), so the first wide line of a pipeline can hitch. No NX 18 game is known to draw wide lines; nothing was measured on the iPhone.
- **Found on:** Mario Golf: Super Rush upstream (no profile in NX 18). Ported from upstream NXNative `7eaf79116` (PR #2510).
- **Check:** `t_wline` (22 checks on the GPU: lists, loop, strip, indexed with restart and base vertex, 8-bit indices, instances, an endpoint behind the eye, the multisampled rectangle), `test_dmsh_pull.py`.

### Indirect draws and dispatches read by the GPU

- **Symptom:** indirect draws whose record the GPU writes earlier in the frame cannot be read on the CPU at replay; GPU-driven effects drawn as indirect quads, which Metal has no primitive for.
- **Cause:** `nvnCommandBufferDrawArraysIndirect`'s record `{count, instances, first, base instance}` has the layout of `MTLDrawPrimitivesIndirectArguments` (and `DrawElementsIndirect`'s `{count, instances, first index, base vertex, base instance}` that of `MTLDrawIndexedPrimitivesIndirectArguments`), but quads, fans and indexed draws with 8-bit indices have no Metal form.
- **Fix:** the replay issues Metal indirect draws from the pool buffer. `ixc_run` in `nvn_mtl.m` converts records of quads, fans and indexed ones with 8-bit indices with a compute pass into indexed indirect arguments in the frame arena, and the draw indexes the shared quad / fan index buffers (indexed ones get a 32-bit list expanded on the GPU). A compute pass cannot run inside a render pass, so when the first such draw is met it converts every such draw up to the next buffer write outside a render pass (copy, update, fill, texture → buffer, dispatch; they bump `g_wr_epoch`). Each record is then read after its last writer, and only a draw met inside an already open render pass splits it. `DispatchComputeIndirect` → `dispatchThreadgroupsWithIndirectBuffer`; when the shader reads the driver segment, the GPU copies the counts into c0 + 0x200 first.
- **Found on:** [Hyrule Warriors: Age of Calamity](../../dm/games/01002B00111A2000/README.md) (its GPU-driven effects are indirect quads).
- **Check:** `DM_NVN_IXCLOG=<n>` logs the first n conversions (record → arguments) once the GPU has run them.

### Indirect-count multi-draws

- **Symptom:** an empty G-buffer pass (the title screen showed only the UI); later, the submitting thread waiting for the GPU before each such draw (36–54 fps in Kamura Village on an iPhone). MIO also reported missing level geometry with maxima of 4,594 and 8,192 draws.
- **Cause:** `nvnCommandBufferMultiDrawElementsIndirectCount` / `MultiDrawArraysIndirectCount` were empty. The scene is GPU-driven: compute culling writes the draw records (stride 20) and a u32 count, then up to `max` draws (1–49 per call) are made from them. The original recorder also truncated every call at 1,024 draws, before the GPU-produced count was available.
- **Fix:** calls with a maximum of up to 1,024 are recorded as individual indirect draws. Larger calls store the argument address, stride, count address and maximum in a single command. For ordinary array and indexed draws, a compute pass masks the argument records on the GPU in batches, writing zeros after the live count. Consecutive draws in the same render pass reuse their encoder state. Maxima beyond the mask table capacity use the synchronized count to bound the work to live draws. The recording size for a large call is independent of its maximum.
- **Found on:** [Monster Hunter Rise](../../dm/games/0100B04011742000/README.md) and [MIO: Memories in Orbit, issue #941](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/941). The visibility-buffer shading issue in MIO is still open.
- **Note:** the replay used to commit its work and wait for the GPU before the first such draw after each GPU write, then read the count on the CPU (`mdi_wait`; still used when a CPU count is needed, including quads, fans and 8-bit indices). In Kamura Village that wait held the submitting thread 14 of its 20 ms a frame on an iPhone (36–54 fps). On the Mac the same scene's submit took 20 ms of wall time for 6.1 ms of work, and now 5.1 ms, all of it work.

### Indirect-count multi-draws split the pass an earlier draw opened

- **Symptom:** in a pass dump (`DM_NVN_PASSDUMP`), `M … indirect-count multi-draw masking: render pass split here` inside scene passes, and the periodic `indirect-count multi-draw masking:` line counts `render pass split` times in the tens of thousands. Each split loads the pass's colour and depth again (Mario Kart World: 2048x1152, 20 MB).
- **Cause:** `mdx_run` copies a multi-draw's records at its first draw, and the compute pass that copies them cannot run inside a render pass. When an ordinary draw had already opened the pass, the copy ended it.
- **Fix:** general (`nvn_mtl.m` `mdx_prescan`, ported from upstream NXNative `a4705fe1`): a draw that is not such a multi-draw and opens a pass first runs the same forward scan, once per `g_mdx_epoch` and submission, so the multi-draws after it are copied before the pass. It runs only once the game has drawn such a multi-draw or an indirect mesh draw (`g_mdx_runs`), so other games do not walk their submissions for it. Ranges replayed through `draw_multi` are not scanned ahead and behave as before. On by default, as upstream. `DM_NVN_MDX_PRESCAN=0` turns it off; `=ab` alternates it with `DM_AB` for an A/B in one run; both are kept from `Documents/env.txt` in a player build (`player_env.h`), for the on-battery iPhone A/B. The periodic masking line counts the scans (`scans at a pass's opening`).
- **Draw-written records and counts:** a copy made ahead comes before the draw that opens the pass, and a draw's shader can write a record or visibility count a later multi-draw reads. Both `ORDER_PRIMITIVES` and `ORDER_INDIRECT_DATA` order earlier work before later vertex or indirect fetch (`GAME_BARRIER_FULL`). Either barrier after shader writes since the copies were made (`g_mdx_wr` against `g_vbuf_write_epoch`) bumps `g_mdx_epoch`, and later multi-draws copy again. Before upstream PR #2945 (ported from `5d1010307`) only `ORDER_INDIRECT_DATA` did: Bayonetta 3's depth prepass writes visibility flags after the prescan had copied their old zeros, and its primitive barrier (`0x21`) left those zero draw arguments in use, so its opaque character draws were masked out (upstream's capture and the PR author's Mac and iPhone runs). This also applies to the scan from a multi-draw's own first draw over later draws.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_mdx_prescan`: split counts and pictures for the first multi-draw (split), a pass opened by an ordinary draw (none), count 0, an indirect barrier with and without a shader write before it, and a shader write with no barrier; then a real fragment store of an indexed draw's count (20-byte record stride) followed by an indirect barrier, a primitive barrier, the game's `0x21`, and a primitive barrier with the writer later in the pass (the last three fail without the fix).
- **Result upstream on Mario Kart World 1.8.0** (M4 Pro, upstream's regression route): in pass dumps every 600 frames, 12 and 10 masking splits in two runs without it and 0 in two runs with it. In one run alternating `DM_NVN_MDX_PRESCAN=ab` every 300 frames: over the first ten race phases, attachment loads 69 MB and stores 47 MB a frame lower and 2.2 fewer passes, every phase; over the last fifteen no difference. GPU time unchanged within the noise (p50 about 9.6 ms over the race). Less memory traffic a frame is the expected gain on an iPhone (power, then heat), not GPU time; not yet measured on an iPhone on battery. The other games that draw indirect-count multi-draws or indirect mesh draws (Ocarina of Time's mesh draws, Monster Hunter Rise) change pass structure with it too and have not been run with it, here or upstream: the picture should not change, but they belong in its A/B.
- **Found on:** [Mario Kart World](../../dm/games/0400C3F00006E000/README.md); [Bayonetta 3](../../dm/games/01004A4010FEA000/README.md#rendering) (draw-written visibility counts, upstream).

### CPU reads after a drain wait only for later writes

- **Symptom:** a hitch of a second or more as a level opens: a few frames of 330–540 ms whose submission takes 2–3 times its CPU time, and the wait ledger (`DM_NVN_WAITS=1`) shows thousands of indirect-count multi-draw waits for those frames only.
- **Cause:** a draw with a geometry stage cannot be masked on the GPU (`mdx_run`): the geometry path reads its count, its indirect record and its indices on the CPU, after a drain (`mdi_wait`: commit, wait for the GPU) when the GPU may have written them. Which writes required one was decided too coarsely. `mdi_live` drained after any `g_wr_epoch` bump since the last drain, and the draw before had bumped it by dispatching its own geometry kernel. `geo_cpu_hazard` looked at every GPU write of the submission, including those the drain had completed. So each geometry draw of such a multi-draw drained the GPU once or twice: about 1,900 drains a frame in Donkey Kong Bananza's level build (35,775 draws in one frame).
- **Fix:** a drain completes every GPU write encoded before it, so until the submission ends only writes encoded after it can make a CPU read stale. Those are kept in a short list of ranges from the same sources the submission-wide sets have: storage writes and global-memory writes (`sbw_note`), and copies, updates and fills (`pend_add`). The drain also marks all argument buffers dirty, because `encode_args` notes storage writes only when it encodes a binding, not when it reuses one. The list is trusted only in the drain's submission, until it overflows or a write of unknown size arrives, and while every `g_wr_epoch` bump since the drain came from a geometry kernel, compute or mesh (`g_wr_geo`), whose storage writes are noted as its arguments are encoded. Transform feedback's writes are not, so it falls back to the old rule. `mdi_live` and `geo_cpu_hazard` read without draining when nothing on the list overlaps the bytes read (`drw_valid`, `drw_hits` in `nvn_mtl.m`).
- **Check:** `t_geometry_hazards` covers the rule: earlier writes stop counting after a drain, later storage writes and copies count byte for byte, a geometry kernel keeps the list, another epoch bump, an unknown size or an overflow drops it, adjacent writes merge, and a new submission starts without one. That is 39 checks, passing with early submission on and off; the 1,920-check geometry suite also passes. On Donkey Kong Bananza's scripted route the level opening went from 540 + 433 + 337 ms to one frame of 195–280 ms (the CPU-bound build), and the indirect-count waits from 18.85 to 0.10 a frame in that window. Frames through the opening and the first gameplay show the terrain, rocks, vines and props as before.
- **Found on:** [Donkey Kong Bananza](../../dm/games/040074A01BF12000/README.md#found-on-this-game).

### `nvnCommandBufferDrawTexture`

- **Symptom:** a black screen while the frame loop runs at full rate, with `[RED] NVN unimplemented function called: nvnCommandBufferDrawTexture`; once drawn, the picture alternates frame by frame between the right image and another texture's (a solid colour, a still image).
- **Cause:** the call draws a texture region (texels, through the handle's sampler) into the destination region of the bound colour targets (pixels) without a shader of the game's, and the host had no implementation. Its handle holds IDs of the texture and sampler pools the command buffer bound, as a bind's does: read as IDs of the first pool, a game that draws from a second pool got that pool's textures at the same IDs.
- **Fix:** recorded as `OP_DRAW_TEX` with the handle moved to the bound pools, as `bind_handles` does (`hle_nvn.c`), and replayed by `draw_tex` (`nvn_mtl.m`): its own render pass per bound colour target after the pass in progress, the pending clears landed first, with the scissor and channel mask; the replay's analyses (pass fusion, deferral, clear hoisting, the store lookahead) treat it as a texture copy. Region coordinates map to the targets' rows directly in either window origin, since the host keeps the Switch's memory layout. Blending is not applied (`[RED] DrawTexture with blending on`), and integer, depth and compressed targets are not drawn.
- **Found on:** [Streets of Rage 4](../../dm/games/0100EC9010258000/README.md): its scene target gets the movie frame this way (the intro, the title's flames), from movie textures registered at IDs 256 and 257 of a second texture pool.

### Rasterizer discard preserves vertex storage writes

- **Symptom:** the HUD and sky render but the battle's characters and terrain are absent; the replay logs `draw skipped: rasterizer discard` between model draws.
- **Cause:** the replay returned from every discarded draw without active transform feedback. Rasterizer discard only suppresses rasterization: vertex storage writes and atomics still execute. Metal additionally requires a void-returning vertex function when rasterization is disabled.
- **Fix:** the offline builder adds `main0_discard` to vertex shaders with writable reflected bindings or image stores (`vs_writes` in `dmsh_build.py`: an image declared `access::write` or `access::read_write`). It retains the original body and early returns and drops only the stage outputs. Replay uses that entry in a separate pipeline with no fragment stage and rasterization disabled, with the ordinary array/indexed/instanced draw machinery and GPU-write tracking. Switching discard marks the pipeline dirty; discarded draws cannot prove an attachment fully overwritten. Pure vertex shaders with neither storage or image writes nor active feedback can still be skipped (`draw skipped: rasterizer discard with no vertex storage or image writes`). Transform feedback retains its capture entry.
- **Check:** `t_rast_discard` passes 19 checks under Metal validation: array/indexed storage writes despite an empty scissor and both faces culled, two instances, unchanged clear colours, normal rendering between two discard phases, and an ordered storage-write consumer. `test_discard.py` compiles a generated entry with side effects before two different returns for Mac and iOS, and checks which vertex shaders get one.
- **Found on:** [Xenoblade Chronicles 3](../../dm/games/010074F013262000/README.md#files-and-shaders); its note tracks the gameplay verification.
- **Also found on:** [Metroid Prime Remastered](../../dm/games/010012101468C000/README.md): image stores only. Its volumetric fog fills a 100×57×64 RGBA16F froxel volume with `1cbc19f806a08166`, 5,700 points with rasterization off that each write the 3D image; a full-screen pass (`1b17e5a457135e3b`) then samples the volume at each pixel's depth and blends it over the scene (colour × alpha + inscatter). Only storage buffer writes counted, so the draw was skipped, the volume stayed zero, and every scene (the Frigate's hull, the gunship in the opening) came out black while the HUD drew over it. Neither the CPU nor any other GPU operation writes the volume (its guest memory stays zero, pool flags `0x22`); `DM_NVN_TEXSNAP` showed the scene intact until the fog pass.

### Primitive barriers order vertex storage writes

- **Symptom:** exploding meshes and displaced model parts after vertex-only work; native post-vertex captures can contain valid positions for some meshes while other palettes are incorrect.
- **Cause:** `ORDER_PRIMITIVES` was omitted from the pass-splitting condition after shader storage writes. A primitive barrier orders earlier vertex work before later vertex work, including its storage writes. Metal does not supply that ordering between draws in one render encoder. Xenoblade Chronicles 3 uses barrier `0x21` after vertex shaders update its bone matrices; fragment-only barrier handling left dependent vertex work unordered.
- **Fix:** a primitive barrier after shader writes ends the render pass, as fragment barriers already do. The next encoder's full consumer barrier supplies the required visibility.
- **Check:** `t_rast_discard` passes 19 GPU checks under Metal validation, including a 4,096-vertex storage producer followed by a primitive barrier and a vertex consumer in the same submission.
- **Found on:** [Xenoblade Chronicles 3](../../dm/games/010074F013262000/README.md#files-and-shaders); `boot24barrier` removes the exploding meshes.

### Ordering barriers before a draw that writes

- **Symptom:** a character's mesh stretched for one frame into a long wedge or sheet that reaches another character, and other characters missing in that frame; intermittent (about one frame in a hundred near a group of characters). A pass dump shows `barrier bits 0x1 (render pass open)` before vertex-only draws (`fs=-`, `discard=1`) that bind a storage buffer `rw` which the draws before them read `ro`.
- **Cause:** a barrier with an ordering bit (ORDER_PRIMITIVES, ORDER_FRAGMENTS, ORDER_FRAGMENTS_TILED) also makes the earlier work finish before a later draw writes, so a write cannot reach memory those draws still read. The host ended the pass at such a barrier only when the pass already had shader writes ([Primitive barriers order vertex storage writes](#primitive-barriers-order-vertex-storage-writes)), so a write after it ran in the same Metal pass as the readers, where Metal gives no order between draws. Xenoblade Chronicles 2 skins each character with a vertex-only draw (`shd_post.wishp` var122) that writes its blended bone matrices from slot 0 of one shared 384,000-byte buffer, which the character's draws then read, and puts ORDER_PRIMITIVES before the next character's: one dumped frame had 91 such draws, 43 of them in a pass with the previous character's draws, whose later vertices could read the next character's matrices.
- **Fix:** an ordering barrier in an open pass without shader writes leaves a mark (`g_pass_order`: primitives, fragments), and the next draw whose program writes an image or a storage buffer is ordered after the pass's earlier draws (`order_draw`). When those draws read memory only in their vertex stage (`order_note`: anything bound in a later stage but samplers and driver data counts), or the barrier orders only primitives (NVN leaves the earlier fragments unordered then too), a barrier inside the pass from the vertex stage to the writer's stages does it (`barrierAfterEncoderStages`), and the pass keeps its attachments; otherwise, for a write in a geometry or tessellation stage, or in a merged or deferred pass, the pass ends first (Metal has no barrier from the fragment stage back to the vertex stage inside a pass). A draw without writes changes nothing. Ending the pass instead split Xenoblade Chronicles 2's geometry pass about a hundred times a frame (510 passes instead of 400, GPU p50 8.8 → 10.3 ms at Goldmouth Return Port); the in-pass barrier keeps 400 passes and the GPU time (8.2 to 8.7 ms). `t_rast_discard` checks six cases under Metal validation.
- **Found on:** [Xenoblade Chronicles 2](../../dm/games/0100E95004038000/README.md#rendering): the stretched geometry of GitHub issue #643, at Goldmouth Return Port and in the Argentum Bazaar.

### A barrier after vertex-stage writes keeps the pass

- **Symptom:** in `DM_NVN_PASSDUMP`, a geometry pass ended at every character: `barrier bits 0x1: ends the pass (shader writes)` after each vertex-only skinning draw, then a new pass on the same targets, each loading and storing every attachment.
- **Cause:** a game barrier after shader writes in a pass ended the pass ([Primitive barriers order vertex storage writes](#primitive-barriers-order-vertex-storage-writes)), even when every write of the pass came from vertex stages, which a barrier inside the pass can order.
- **Fix:** at the game barrier, when the open pass's writes all came from vertex stages (`g_pass_swst`, kept by `swrite_note`), the pass goes on behind a barrier from the vertex stage to the vertex and fragment stages (`OP_BARRIER` in `mtl_exec`, `nvn_mtl.m`; `DM_NVN_VBAR_PASS`, on by default; `0` ends the pass as before, `ab` alternates with `DM_AB`). Still ended: a barrier with `ORDER_INDIRECT_DATA` (the vertex stage does not order indirect argument fetch; with shader writes in the pass this bit alone now ends it too, with the switch on or off), a fragment barrier after a draw that read memory in a later stage (`order_note`), writes from any other stage, slice-merged and face-split passes, and a later task or mesh draw (`g_pass_vbar`: the barrier does not order their stages). `prog_swrites` also counts storage written through unknown or queried bindings (`NVN_RB_SBUFU`, `NVN_RB_SBUFQ`).
- **Found on:** upstream, Xenoblade Chronicles: Definitive Edition, Switch 2 Edition ([PR #3374](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/3374)), the Monolith skinning draws. Ported 2026-11-27 from upstream `dc9c2374b`; nothing was measured on a game here yet.
- **Check:** `check_t_rast_discard` (54 checks, with and without `DM_NVN_VBAR_PASS`): one pass instead of two after a vertex write and a primitive barrier, two cycles across a same-target continuation keep one pass and the values the consumer reads, and `ORDER_INDIRECT_DATA` (with or without `ORDER_PRIMITIVES`), fragment-stage writes and a fragment barrier after a fragment-stage read each end the pass. The `[nvn] pass merges` line counts the barriers kept per frame.

### Transform feedback captured by a vertex entry point

- **Symptom:** characters collapsed into one thin spike (vertex streams read from feedback buffers that were never written); a character covered by a solid box while it moves (its fur shells pulled toward lagged positions that were never written); a pass dump shows draws with a vertex shader and no fragment shader (`fs=-`) before the draws that read them, and `Y tf begin … written 0/0/0/0` with no `T … tf capture` line.
- **Cause:** a game that skins on the GPU with transform feedback: a vertex-only program runs under `SetRasterizerDiscard` between `BeginTransformFeedback` / `EndTransformFeedback` and writes into feedback buffers that later draws read as vertex streams. The host ignored all of it, so the streams kept whatever was there. Metal has no feedback stage.
- **Fix:**
  - the layout comes from the program's control section (`tf_layout` in `dmsh_build.py`): +0x6dc u16 strides, +0x6e8 per-buffer output counts, +0x6ec buffer count, and per buffer b the output locations (0x20 + n) at +0xdc + 0x80 b and matching **byte** component counts at +0x4dc + 0x80 b. Multiple outputs are interleaved in that order. Unknown forms get a red line and no capture entry;
  - `main0_tf` (`vs_tf`) retains the body and stores the output bits into Metal vertex buffers 8–11, with the GPU-prepared capture record at buffer 14. The reflection lists `entry_tf` and `tf_strides`. Geometry programs give their output reader the same entry; it gathers the packed point/line/triangle indices while writing linear feedback records;
  - `nvn_feedback.inc` keeps offsets and counts on the GPU. Begin clears them; End/Pause save them in the game's opaque 32-byte control buffer; Resume restores them. A compute helper bounds each capture by all active streams and complete primitives. `DrawTransformFeedback` uses the saved count as indirect array-draw arguments. Captures, counter copies and consuming draws follow encoder ordering, and captured ranges enter GPU-write tracking. Plain vertex capture supports array point/line/triangle lists with or without rasterizer discard, including instances; indexed/indirect vertex capture remains open. Geometry capture uses the existing general assembly and compute path. `DM_NVN_PASSDUMP` prints the records as `Y tf …` and array captures as `T … tf capture`.
- **Check:** `test_transform_feedback.py` compiles generated vertex/geometry entries for Mac and iOS; `t_transform_feedback` checks interleaving, saved offsets, resume, bounded capture, GPU-produced geometry counts, complete primitives and a following draw that consumes the saved count in the same submission.
- **Found on:** [Pokémon Legends: Z-A](../../dm/games/0100F43008C44000/README.md): the character preview. 28 such shaders (e.g. `681cc42e48c0bbc0` in the `special/imgui.trsha` pack; skin indices in attribute 12, weights in 13) write positions (location 0 xyz, 12 bytes a vertex) and packed normal and tangent bits (locations 1 and 2, x only, 4 bytes) into three feedback buffers; the character draws read them as vertex streams 0–2 (`RGB32F`, `RGB10A2SN`, `RGB10A2SN`). Every buffer in this game holds one output from x on (strides 12/4/4, 12/12/12, 12/4, 12). The game draws these while its frame target is bound. The pass dump showed a pass of 19 draws with `fs=-` before the character pass. Tears of the Kingdom's 14,766 and Ace Attorney's 2,475 vertex shaders have no feedback at all.
  - [Donkey Kong Country: Tropical Freeze](../../dm/games/0100C1F0051B6000/README.md): DK's fur. While DK moved, his fur shells (an instanced draw, `648304c43bbe3463` / `5b5ffba62bca9e0a`, three shells on the body's index buffer) covered him as one solid box. Each frame a vertex-only draw (`6979dd3cdbd62373` / `7dcdaed991c74bfe`, 3,400 vertices) captures the fur's lagged positions into one buffer, two outputs per vertex (xyz each, stride 24; output count 2, component bytes `03 03`), and the shells read them as vertex attribute 6. Two things kept the buffer at zero (`DM_NVN_KEYDUMP=5b5ffba62bca9e0a`: stream 6 held zeros and `2.3e24`): the old one-output rule rejected the layout (`[RED] … transform feedback buffer 0 has a layout of unknown form`), and the game draws the capture with rasterization on (no fragment shader, colour mask 0, no `SetRasterizerDiscard`), which the host did not capture. Four of its 11,644 vertex shaders have this form; over Z-A's and this game's 23,277 vertex shaders together, the old and new readings give identical layouts and capture code for all 28 shaders the old rule took.

- **Found on:** [Xenoblade Chronicles 3](../../dm/games/010074F013262000/README.md#files-and-shaders): effect vertex and geometry programs use interleaved outputs, including eleven outputs in a 172-byte stream, and draw from saved feedback counts.

### Geometry-shader draws encoded a run at a time

- **Symptom:** a scene drawn with a geometry shader per mesh runs at 5 to 15 fps on the Mac, with the submitting thread or the GPU taking most of the frame.
- **Cause:** the general geometry replay (`nvn_geometry.inc`) handled each draw on its own. It waited for the GPU before every indexed draw (the indices are assembled on the CPU), ended the open render pass, and ran four encoders: the vertex capture, the geometry kernel, the index compaction in one GPU thread, and the raster draw reopening the pass with its loads.
- **Fix:**
  - CPU index assembly waits when pending or current/prior GPU writes, unknown ranges or sparse aliases prevent a safe read (`geo_index_wait`); native capture retains GPU ordering, and unique capture indices use a bounded bitset instead of `qsort`;
  - the compaction (`dm/gfx/msl/geo_pack.metal`) uses three blocked parallel prefix-scan stages (`geo_pack_count`, `geo_pack_scan`, `geo_pack_write`) with threadgroup barriers;
  - the first geometry draw of a run looks ahead (`geo_run_walk`, with state replay only) across the geometry draws that follow it with only state, query reports and event signals in between. It encodes all their vertex captures in one render pass and all their kernels and compactions in one compute pass. Each later draw of the run then only rasterizes its prepared output in the open render pass, omitting inactive colour attachments across the run when `geo_color_omit` holds.
- **Found on:** [NieR:Automata The End of YoRHa Edition](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md) and [US 1.0.3](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md): about 70 index-build draws a frame in the flight section and up to 932 in heavy outdoor combat.

### Helper encoders end the open copy encoder first

- **Symptom:** `SIGSEGV` in `-[AGXG18GFamilyComputeContext_mtlnext performEndEncoding]` (from `rp_begin_ex_`, or in its `dealloc` on the encoding thread); Metal validation (`MTL_DEBUG_LAYER=1 MTL_DEBUG_LAYER_ERROR_MODE=nslog`) names it: "Command buffer already has an open command encoder".
- **Cause:** `tf_draw` opened its render encoder while the copy encoder (`g_blit`) was open, which the inline updates `ubd_land` copies there leave behind, as does a copy or a dispatch just before it.
- **Fix:** every other helper pass ends `g_blit` before it opens its own encoder, and now `tf_draw` does too (GitHub issue #101).
- **Found on:** [Pokémon Legends: Z-A](../../dm/games/0100F43008C44000/README.md): two minutes into a new game, about frame 3390 on the Mac, with the encoding thread on and off.

### Viewport swizzles applied as a flipped viewport

- **Symptom:** the whole picture upside down (Absolum's title logo and "Press Any Button" mirrored top to bottom); a red-reporting stub for `nvnCommandBufferSetViewportSwizzles`.
- **Cause:** the game keeps NVN's lower-left window origin (handled as for Link's Awakening, [Lower-left window origin](#lower-left-window-origin)) and, twice a frame, sets viewport 0's swizzle with `nvnCommandBufferSetViewportSwizzles` to (x, −y, z, w), which negates clip-space y: how a renderer with OpenGL heritage flips its picture.
- **Fix:** the swizzle is recorded (`OP_VP_SWIZZLE`) and applied at the draw as a viewport flipped about its centre, the same mapping. Facing is decided after the swizzle on the GPU as in Metal, so the winding stays. Other swizzles (axis exchanges, negated x / z / w) give a red line and are drawn unswizzled.
- **Found on:** [Absolum](../../dm/games/0100A83022A22000/README.md) (FNA3D's NVN driver, `FNA3D_Driver_NVN.cpp`).

### Black shapes on sprites when Unreal's GPU work runs late

- **Symptom:** on an Unreal Engine game with a full GPU, moving sprites flicker black for a frame: cut straight across, or a whole silhouette in the sprite's outline. It follows GPU time against the frame budget, and goes away at 30 fps or with GPU time to spare. The black is already in the scene colour the base pass writes.
- **Cause:** a race in the engine's NVN code. A dynamic buffer keeps `nvn.NumBufferedFrames` copies (3), and each write lock moves to the next one with no fence. The render thread can come round to a copy while draws queued for the GPU still read it. If that lands between a sprite's depth prepass and its base pass, the two see different vertices: the base pass tests depth `EQUAL`, draws nothing where they differ, and the cleared black stays.
- **Fix:** a workaround, since the missing fence is in the game's code: the rewriter gives each dynamic buffer a fourth copy (`dm/tools/ue_ring.py`, called by `patch_nso.py`), with no build ID, on any main where its chain holds. A buffer has room for exactly 4. The variable itself cannot be set to 4: its other readers size fixed tables, and the deferred-delete table has room for 3, so only the constructor's load changes. Open: this adds one lock of margin and no fence, so a GPU further behind, or a buffer locked more than once a frame, can still reach a queued copy. A single comparison has been run (5 black frames against 0 at equal load), and hit counts vary between runs. The fallback is copying small vertex streams at encode, which gave 0 against 10 and 14 in a diagnostic build; it costs every game a copy per draw and does not cover index buffers.
- **Found on:** [TRIANGLE STRATEGY](../../dm/games/0100CC80140F8000/README.md), [issue #1040](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1040).

## Uniforms, copies and ordering

### Bindless handles read from guest memory directly

- **Symptom:** a `sample` of the submit thread shows `ubo_read` calling `-[MTLBuffer contents]` with `objc_retainAutorelease` at every handle read, and `objc_autoreleasePoolPop` at commit draining those buffers. Fire Emblem Fortune's Weave: about 4 % of the submit thread in the reads and as much again in the drain.
- **Cause:** `contents` is an inner-pointer method, so under ARC each call retains and autoreleases the buffer; a bindless shader reads dozens of handles a draw. The argument writes in `encode_args_` asked the arena's buffer for its contents the same way.
- **Fix:** `ubo_read` in `nvn_mtl.m`, for every game. A pool's buffer wraps guest memory without a copy at its own address (`mtl_pool_add` is the only place pools are made), so once `mtl_addr` finds the pool, the bytes are read at the guest address itself; `mtl_addr` still runs, so the pool check and its residency are unchanged. An arena keeps its buffer's `contents` (`NvArena.cpu`), and the argument, push-constant, driver-segment and border writes go through it. If pools ever stop being no-copy wrappers, this read would need the buffer again.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> run`. Fortune's Weave's battle map (MacBook Pro M1 Pro): submit 42.8k → 39.2k instructions per draw.
- **Found on:** [Fire Emblem Fortune's Weave](../../dm/games/04007EF01F8AE000/README.md).

### Depth plane copies between depth formats

- **Symptom:** `[RED] texture→texture copy case not implemented: 1920x1080x1→1920x1080x1 flags 0 format 260→252 3D 0→0`.
- **Cause:** the game copies its depth-stencil target (Metal `Depth32Float_Stencil8`, 260) into a depth-only texture (`Depth32Float`, 252) of the same size. A Metal blit copies only between equal formats, and the draw path does not take depth formats.
- **Fix:** such a copy, either way between the two formats, goes through a staging buffer kept from copy to copy: the 32-bit float depth is copied out of the source with `MTLBlitOptionDepthFromDepthStencil` and into the destination's depth plane the same way (`nvn_mtl.m`, the texture copy).
- **Found on:** [EA SPORTS FC 27](../../dm/games/0100C49025D3E000/README.md) (Frostbite): from the title on.

### Depth plane copies on the encode thread

- **Symptom:** `FATAL deferred encoding: DmRecEnc has no stand-in for -copyFromTexture:sourceSlice:sourceLevel:sourceOrigin:sourceSize:toBuffer:destinationOffset:destinationBytesPerRow:destinationBytesPerImage:options:` with Metal encoding on its own thread (`DM_NVN_ENC_THREAD=1`, the default on iOS), at the first [depth plane copy](#depth-plane-copies-between-depth-formats).
- **Cause:** that copy is the only one that passes blit options (`MTLBlitOptionDepthFromDepthStencil`, `enc_b_copy_texture_buffer_opt` and `enc_b_copy_buffer_texture_opt` in `nvn_mtl.m`), and the encode thread's recorder (`DmRecEnc` in `nvn_rec.h`) had stand-ins only for the copies without options. A call it has no stand-in for stops the host.
- **Fix:** `nvn_rec.h` records both copies with their options (`RO_C_COPY_TB_OPT`, `RO_C_COPY_BT_OPT`) and replays them on the Metal 4 compute encoder. Every selector the host's encoder wrappers send now has a stand-in. `check_t_rt_range` also runs with `DM_NVN_ENC_THREAD=1`; `t_rt_range` and `t_unwritten_store` stopped there before.
- **Found on:** the host's tests. [PR #930](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/930) added the texture-to-buffer stand-in for [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md) on the Mac with the encode thread on (not run here).

### Indirect task / mesh draws on the encode thread

- **Symptom:** `FATAL deferred encoding: DmRecEnc has no stand-in for -drawMeshThreadgroupsWithIndirectBuffer:threadsPerObjectThreadgroup:threadsPerMeshThreadgroup:`, then `CRASH sig=6` under `mtk_encode`, with Metal encoding on its own thread (`DM_NVN_ENC_THREAD=1`, the default on iOS), at the first [indirect task / mesh draw](shaders.md#task-and-mesh-shaders-in-bnsh-and-indirect-mesh-draws).
- **Cause:** `nvn_geometry.inc` encodes an indirect task / mesh draw with `drawMeshThreadgroupsWithIndirectBuffer:…`, and the encode thread's recorder (`DmRecEnc` in `nvn_rec.h`) had a stand-in only for the direct one (`drawMeshThreadgroups:…`). A call it has no stand-in for stops the host.
- **Fix:** `nvn_rec.h` records the indirect draw (`RO_R_MESH_IND`: the argument buffer's GPU address and the threads per object and mesh threadgroup, as `RO_R_MESH` keeps them) and replays it. `t_rec_draws` checks that the recorder has a stand-in for every draw the NVN layer encodes.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md): the iOS app aborted when a save loaded; on the Mac with the encode thread on, 28 s after the title ([issue #1265](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1265)).

### The GPU idles before each submission while the replay waits for the encode thread

- **Symptom:** with Metal encoding on its own thread (`DM_NVN_ENC_THREAD=1`, the default on iOS), a frame's GPU span is far above its GPU work, and the gaps sit before the first encoder of a submission: `DM_NVN_GPUPROF` with its CPU timeline shows the GPU done with everything committed while the next submission is still being encoded. The wait ledger (`DM_NVN_WAITS=1`) shows `encode thread drain` blocked about once per submission.
- **Cause:** `mtl_sub_commit` waited for the encode thread to finish the submission (`rec_drain`) before returning, so the replay never translated the next submission while the encode thread encoded the last one, and the GPU got each submission only once both had finished it. A game that splits its frame at every event signal makes many submissions (each `nvnQueueSubmitCommands` is cut into one per wait, signal and fence by the queue scheduler), and a submission under `cut_draws()` draws has no [segment](frame-rate.md#submissions-handed-to-the-gpu-in-segments) to start the GPU early.
- **Fix** (upstream b2ae958f2): the submission's records go to the encode thread with a note of its fence (`RO_NOTE` on `g_sub_cmt`), and the replay goes on. Everything else that goes to the queue after it is a record too (the next submission's commits, upload batches, snapshot copies, one-off command buffers in order, sparse mappings), a CPU wait for the GPU waits for the fence's signal, which comes after the commit, and graphics suspend waits for the last fence behind an ordered one-off command buffer (`drain_steps`: `once_drain`, a record whose commit sends any held submissions first, then `mtl_finish_gpu`). The one direct commit that reads the game's work is the display command buffer of a present without a snapshot: it records a flush of the held submissions at its place (`rec_flush_held`, `RO_FLUSH`; [held submissions](frame-rate.md#a-frames-submissions-queued-behind-its-first-one-while-the-iphones-gpu-clock-is-low)) and waits for the note of the submissions before it (`sub_commit_wait`, `pq_item.sub_v`), beside NX 18's note for an upload batch the present itself flushed (`pup_commit_wait`). `DM_NVN_SUB_DRAIN=1` brings the wait back (`ab` alternates with `DM_AB`).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) on upstream's iPhone 17 (Kokiri Forest): about 30 submissions a frame; one frame spanned 34.9 ms for 20.2 ms of GPU work, and 13.6 of its 14.75 ms of gaps came before a submission's first encoder; the replay thread waited for the encode thread 36 times a frame, 10 ms in all. Not measured on NX 18.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_sub_overlap` holds the encode thread inside a submission (a queue block) and checks that the submission returns, that its note waits for the encode thread's commit, and that a present's wait holds until then (with `DM_NVN_SUB_DRAIN=1` the first of those fails). `check_t_present_crop` and `check_t_present_snap` run presents with and without the encode thread and snapshots.

### Mesh draws with no task shader above 1024 workgroups

- **Symptom:** a GPU page fault (`kIOGPUCommandBufferCallbackErrorPageFault`) 30 to 40 s after a save loads, then the game hangs or [stops](#a-gpu-whose-access-macos-revoked-stops-the-game). `DM_NVN_SKIP_MESH=1` makes it go away.
- **Cause:** Metal allows at most 1024 threadgroups in a mesh grid. The object grid has no such limit. A mesh draw with no task shader has no object function, so its workgroup count becomes the mesh grid. Indirect mesh draws reached 1,771 workgroups, direct ones at most 32. A mesh grid above 1024 reads out of bounds. Draws with a task shader are safe: `dmsh_meshstage.py` clamps the grid they emit to `METAL_MAX_GRID`.
- **Fix:** a mesh shader with no task shader gets an object function of its own (`dmsh_meshstage.py` `main0_o`, the library's `entry_object`). Each of its one-thread threadgroups launches a block of up to `DMSH_MESH_GRID` (1024) mesh threadgroups and passes the block's first workgroup in its payload, and the mesh shader takes its workgroup as that plus its threadgroup ID. So the draw stays one Metal draw of ceil(count / 1024) object threadgroups, with no limit of its own: `mtk_encode` computes that for a direct draw, and for an indirect one `mdx_mask` writes it beside the task shader's `{count, 1, 1}`. The task and object functions read the draw's `{count, first}` at buffer 29 (`DMSH_MESH_DRAW_SLOT`): an indirect draw's record itself, a direct draw's copy. `t_mesh_first_use` draws 3,072 workgroups in three blocks, directly and indirectly, each to its own pixel. The object stage costs nothing measurable: Ocarina of Time's `kokiri-deck` route on an M4 Pro held 60 fps at GPU p50 12.0-13.2 ms against 11.8-13.4 ms without it, 3 runs each. An earlier version of the fix, which split the draw into 8 Metal draws of at most 1024 (pull request #1708), cost nothing measurable either, but stopped at 8,192 workgroups.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) on an M2 Max ([issue #1326](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1326), pull request #1708): the foliage's mesh draws. With a pipeline archive state that faulted every time at about frame 1,930, the game ran 14 minutes (frame 30,600) without a fault with the split. On an M4 Pro the Kokiri Forest deck route has about 19 such draws a frame, up to 1,056 workgroups, 28 above 1024 in a run, without a fault on main. PRAGMATA's mesh-only grids reach 6,623 (a test of the split in the pull request).

### Inline uniform updates as ordered GPU copies

- **Cause:** reading the uniform buffer from the CPU at encode time gives the frame's *last* value, not the draw's.
- **Fix:** inline uniform updates (`CommandBufferUpdateUniformBuffer`) are replayed as in-order GPU copies (their order inside one encoder: [Metal 4 does not order operations inside one encoder](#metal-4-does-not-order-operations-inside-one-encoder); inside a pass: [Inline uniform updates inside a render pass](#inline-uniform-updates-inside-a-render-pass)). They are snapshotted at encode time (`mtl_sub_update`): the data is copied into a host-owned ring instead of being read from command memory when the GPU runs.
- **Found on:** [Super Mario Bros. Wonder](../../dm/games/010015100B514000/README.md).

### Pending clears land before a compute dispatch

- **Symptom:** a scene opens near white and fades to its normal colours over several seconds, at each scene change, while the settled picture is right.
